## AI Research

### [Orienting to 1 year AGI timelines](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/biFHYdJnMSCmW9Xou/orienting-to-1-year-agi-timelines)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Summary</span></h1><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Timelines to superintelligence without any slowdown interventions look very short now, perhaps with a median in early 2028. AI will likely dramatically automate and accelerate AI research in 2027.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This means that in a substantial share of worlds where the leading AGI company doesn’t solve alignment during an intelligence explosion, avoiding catastrophe requires aggressive pacing or a pause on development in the next 6-12 months.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If this is true, by far the most promising approaches to reducing the risk of catastrophe for nontechnical people (and plausibly many technical people) seem to be:</span><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lab advocacy:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Lab-facing work that is designed to achieve aggressive pacing or a pause </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">without government involvement</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in the next 6-12 months.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">California lobbying:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Policy advocacy targeting the California state government that is designed to get a law passed or an executive order issued that implements aggressive pacing or a pause within 6-12 months.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Federal lobbying:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> If there’s a major incident roughly an order of magnitude more severe than the Hugging Face incident in the next 6 months, then lobbying Congress or the Trump administration to respond to such an event with aggressive pacing or a pause. </span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In my opinion, </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">most nontechnical AI safety people are not currently pursuing theories of change that are likely to pay off in the next 6-12 months</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. They should probably instead do one of the three strategies I describe above (lab advocacy, California lobbying, or federal lobbying) with a goal of achieving aggressive pacing or pausing within 6-12 months. </span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Among technical work, the most promising approaches seem to be:</span><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For third parties:</span></b><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">laying the technical foundations for a pause: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">assessments measuring AI progress, AI R&amp;D automation, and compute allocation.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">establishing evidence of risk: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">embedded assessments investigating incidents, misalignment, loss-of-control, and covert capabilities.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For people at the labs: cooperating with third-parties in laying the technical foundations for a pause and establishing evidence of risk </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">by</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">giving them as much access and staff-time as they need.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We should have some people working on alignment and control (especially for the early automated researchers) in case ASI does happen soon</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This includes not only alignment/control research but also increasing general willingness-to-pay for safety within labs, both in terms of compute allocation and other tradeoffs between safety and capabilities (e.g. preserving CoT monitorability).</span></li></ul></li></ul></li></ul><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Timelines to ASI are very short now</span></h1><p><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The METR report on AI R&amp;D automation within Anthropic</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is substantial evidence that timelines to superintelligence are now less than 2 years, even accounting for uncertainty about takeoff modeling, the reliability of the report’s conclusions, and other types of uncertainty. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ryan Greenblatt gives a straightforward way to extrapolate METR’s findings in</span><a href="https://x.com/RyanGreenblatt/status/2118447235289817503"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a tweet</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. He claims that “In AI 2027, [the current level of speedup by RSI] happens around March 2027.” This means that </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">real-world AI progress on AI R&amp;D capabilities is faster than in AI 2027, an already extremely bullish scenario.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Given that in AI 2027, ASI is achieved in December 2027, this suggests superintelligence would be developed, without slowdown, around July 2027 (assuming that the original AI 2027 scenario modeled the shape of AI takeoff correctly).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think the most compelling evidence for very short timelines has been the reactions of established forecasters and researchers. In response to the METR report, the AI Futures Project released an emergency</span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/p/q35-timelines-update-looks-like-the"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Q3.5 timelines update</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Here are the all-things-considered forecasts of the team (I note that ASI is generally modeled as being reached less than a year after TED-AI absent deliberate slowdowns):</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794527648/lexical_client_uploads/zgaotulnx2mqkwewdrpf.png" /><figcaption><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/f9PajMLmBYzmWzyDv/q3-5-timelines-update-looks-like-the-intelligence-explosion" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These forecasts</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> give us a very tight deadline to get a pause in place.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Other reactions:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sentinel, an offshoot of the Samotsvety forecasting team (among the best forecasting teams ever) has</span><a href="https://blog.sentinel-team.org/p/anthropic-warns-of-imminent-dramatically"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> updated</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on the METR report to a median arrival date for superintelligence of February 2028. </span></li><li value="2"><a href="https://www.planned-obsolescence.org/p/ai-is-dramatically-accelerating-ai"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ajeya Cotra</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: “If we take it at face value that AI is now nearly doubling the rate of AI capabilities progress and assume acceleration from AI is smooth, this naively implies that we would reach extreme superintelligence within a year.”</span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://x.com/TomDavidsonX/status/2118784742535414033"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tom Davidson</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: “I don’t know whether METR are right. I wish more info was public. But this is exactly what I’d expect to see if there were going to be an intelligence explosion in 2027.”</span></li><li value="4"><a href="https://x.com/JakeMendel99"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jake Mendel</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: “I’ve always had substantially longer timelines than my friends at Anthropic. [...] I now think that we are more than 50% likely to have full blown superintelligence in the next 18 months, absent a slowdown. I need to call my family.”</span></li><li value="5"><a href="https://x.com/austinc3301/status/2119914259232776458"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Agus</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: “RSI is deeply confusing. Timelines are very sensitive to the parameters of your takeoff models, and we’re very uncertain about some of these. Still, I’m left with something like a 25% chance of ASI by the end of 2027 (~50% by end of 2028), assuming no pacing or policy [intervention].”</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic updated its S-1 on November 10 to say that it has reached the threshold from its</span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/files/4zrzovbb/website/0bacdc8440ea96e62a8766d99ebe1d4eea6d5f3a.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> RSP</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (pages 8-9) where it “observe[s] or expect[s] </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">double the rate of progress in AI aggregate capabilities</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” in a way that “it is plausible that this doubling is substantially attributable to the automation of research and/or engineering.” Anthropic</span><a href="https://x.com/theobearman/status/2120124069806756238/photo/1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> writes</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: "Because the AI research and development capability threshold under our Responsible Scaling Policy applies where we ‘observe or expect’ a specified degree of accelerated progress, we made a determination that we have met this threshold based on what we expect from future models and our overall rate of progress, rather than on what we have observed from current models.”</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As a result of the above, my current all-things-considered median date for superintelligence without a slowdown or other major intervention is around March 2028. That is around a year and a half from now.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Three paths to pausing or pacing within 6-12 months</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we achieve ASI within 6-12 months, I estimate that the chance of an AI takeover is roughly 75%. (In a recent</span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-Ef0ocEoBCs"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> talk</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Paul Christiano said it would be a coin flip.) Paired with the timelines above, unless we achieve aggressive pacing or a pause within 6-12 months, we incur something like a 1-in-4 to 1-in-3 chance of AI takeover within 12 months. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is obviously unacceptable. I believe there are three feasible paths to achieving pacing or pausing within 6-12 months, and that these should be the overwhelming priority of the AI safety community right now:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lab advocacy: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Convincing Anthropic, OpenAI, and possibly Meta/Google to implement aggressive pacing or a pause within 6-12 months </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">without government involvement</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">California state action: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Through the California state legislature or an executive order by the Governor, force the major AI developers to implement aggressive pacing or a pause within 6-12 months.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Federal lobbying</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Through Congress (with a veto-proof majority) or the President, force the major AI companies to aggressively pace or pause within 6-12 months. In my view, this option is intractable unless there is a major incident roughly an order of magnitude more salient than the Hugging Face incident (roughly, an</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cxuzALcmucCndYv4a/daniel-kokotajlo-s-shortform?commentId=Cbzx6T3K5vzBwGkpg"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Act of God</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Each of these should aim for pacing or pausing that gets us at the very least a 1 year slowdown in the expected date of superintelligence, such as three of the proposals in the AI Futures Project’s</span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/p/how-to-pace-the-us-frontier"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> How to Pace the U.S. Frontier</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: a 10% AI R&amp;D compute cap, a 9-month capability for AI R&amp;D lag, or a 1-year inference-only pause. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are ongoing efforts in each of these three categories. However, there are several issues with those efforts:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lack of staff.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I estimate that, within the AI safety community, there are roughly: 20 FTEs working on lab advocacy (not counting lab employees), 15 FTEs working on CA state action, and 100 FTEs working on federal lobbying (broadly construed). A large number of nontechnical people in the AI safety community are working on projects that, in my view, do not matter as much given the current timelines situation. These include work on export controls, community building, think tank-style governance research, and (possibly) adversary-facing security. </span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lack of focus on 6-12 month timelines.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Even the people already working in these three areas are mostly not pursuing strategies that are likely to pay off within 6-12 months. For example, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">many </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">people working in and around the federal government work on export controls (which are not very important on 6-12 month timelines), untargeted influence seeking (even in areas outside of AI), or other vague efforts that are unlikely to pay off soon (or might even be net-negative or cancel each other out under short timelines).</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lack of inter-strategy coherence.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The three strategies have obvious synergies. For example, it’s important for those lobbying the federal government to try to prevent the federal government from overriding pacing or a pause created through lab advocacy or California. However, people pursuing each strategy are not yet coordinating enough. For example, many DC people know very little about the state of lab advocacy.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lack of urgency. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many people, even in these categories, are still pursuing vague strategies based on what topics feel fun or interesting, or based on very little actual backchaining. Others are not pursuing anything resembling a strategy. If timelines are really as short as I claim, we have months, not years, to solve these problems. This demands a logistical orientation towards high levels of productivity and urgency. </span><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://slatestarcodex.com/2014/03/24/should-you-reverse-any-advice-you-hear/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Equal and opposite advice</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: people sometimes enter states of panic where they just want to do </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">something</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and their decisionmaking becomes pretty bad and their projects suboptimal.</span></li></ul></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They don’t look on track to succeed. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One individual working in lab advocacy estimated in private conversation that there is a 20-30% chance of pacing/pausing without government involvement happening in the next 6 months. This is not high enough. I estimate the chance of pacing/pausing via the California legislature in the next 10 months (and that is not struck down within weeks by a judge) is roughly 20%. Through federal channels, I estimate 10%. These probabilities are correlated with each other, since the chances of success increase for each channel if there is a major incident, and due to other shared factors. In other words, we are not on track to succeed.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m not necessarily saying that, in some overall sense, lab advocacy, California lobbying, or federal lobbying are neglected on the margin. My claim is that lab advocacy, California lobbying, and federal lobbying </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">with the goal of starting aggressive pacing/pausing within the next 6-12 months</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is greatly neglected. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Any sort of pause that can last more than a year will eventually need to become a global pause. Entering a US-only pause is an important stepping stone towards entering a global pause, but </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I expect pressure from middle powers to be pretty important for the pause going global</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I think some of the best work here amounts to improving the situational awareness of middle power governments and helping them navigate the situation with regards to the US approaching ASI. </span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Technical work that seems most valuable</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think there are a few main categories of work that technical people should focus on:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Making the pause more likely through embedded assessments,</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> which splits into:</span><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Establishing evidence of risk:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Incident investigations, alignment assessments, and assessments focused on monitoring and covert capabilities could all build common knowledge that the current situation is on-track to result in catastrophe, and we urgently need to pause. </span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This work is best done through embedded assessments – consider that the two main pieces of evidence that increased people’s risk estimates in 2026 (the OpenAI-Hugging Face investigation, the METR Anthropic AI R&amp;D assessment) were done by METR in close collaboration with an AI company.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some of this work can still be done without collaborating with labs (e.g. swarmchasing).</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Laying the technical foundations for a pause: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">expanded upon below.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Working on backup alignment plans in case a pause doesn’t happen: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There is a notable chance that a pause never happens, and that we get RSI very soon. I think that alignment work can cash out in two main things:</span><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Aligning and controlling the early automated researchers: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This seems like the most important part to me, given if we mess up on alignment/control with early automated researchers, we might be done for. (Although it’s possible some work of this kind is actually net-negative if not paired with good epistemics, because it would make the situation seem less risky and therefore a pause less likely.)</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Relatedly, we should find ways to increase willingness-to-pay for safety during takeoff, including but not limited to:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">getting allocated more compute relative to capabilities research,</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">getting concessions that might necessitate some capabilities/commercial sacrifices, e.g. preserving CoT monitorability.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My guess is that a large subset of current alignment work isn’t properly oriented towards aligning and controlling the early automated researchers, just from the fact that it presumably wasn’t prioritized with this goal in mind.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figuring out what the early automated researchers should be tasked with: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This one seems much less promising to me – it doesn’t seem like this can meaningfully be done in advance because whatever experiments and decisions we might want to make in advance, we can make much cheaper with better automation. Also, if there is a strong taste bottleneck with early automated researchers, such work (writing Google docs about research agendas) seems like it has strongly diminishing returns right now.</span></li></ul></li></ul><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Laying the technical foundations for a pause</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All three paths to pausing would be substantially helped by technical work focused on setting the foundations for a global pause. I think the most urgent work of this kind is third-party assessments focused on the type of investigation that would be necessary to build trust between participants of a pause. If we get practice doing lower-stakes and lower-scale versions of assessments, we can build buy-in that we could pull off a global pause.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ideally, sometime in the next few months, third parties would conduct assessments with the following properties:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Measuring things relevant to upholding a pause:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Assessments focused on compute allocation and RSI-relevant metrics (e.g. algorithmic efficiency, uplift measures, spend on AI agents, analysis of success rates on internal tasks) seem most relevant for creating the right kind of technical competency and relationships.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Commitment-focused:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Ideally, third parties would verify a claim that a company made about a commitment they intend to uphold.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">High-access:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Assessors would ideally get however much access is given to employees that track similar issues.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cross-company:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> If a third-party assessment simultaneously involves multiple AGI companies, this setup is more similar to the kind of setup we might want during a global pause.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">International: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Getting practice verifying claims between the US and China seems extremely valuable, although it might be out of scope for current third-party orgs without explicit endorsement from the US government. US-UK or US-EU collaborations could provide some lower-stakes practice.</span></li><li value="6"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Good process transparency terms: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Without proper </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-10-14-our-approach-to-risk-assessment/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">process transparency</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (being able to say what access was given, what was redacted, whether the redactions were material to the conclusions), it will be hard to build trust.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I talk more about the technical work that would facilitate a pause in my recent post “</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/iMKAvHSWxSoDpBoi6/we-urgently-need-to-practice-before-we-can-pause"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We urgently need to practice before we can pause</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.”</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What should you do?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As I write above, I think for most people the most impactful path is to do lab advocacy, lobby the California legislature, lobby the federal government, establish evidence of risk, lay the technical foundations for a pause, or work on aligning and controlling early automated researchers. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here are some heuristics to help you decide which of these you should do:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For policy people:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do I have a large number of relationships with Anthropic or OpenAI employees? If so, I should probably move to SF and do lab advocacy aimed at pacing/pausing within 6-12 months.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Am I a Republican? If so, I should probably move to DC and lobby the federal government (Congress or the administration) aiming for pacing/pausing within 6-12 months if there is a major incident. </span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Am I a Democrat? If so, I should probably move to California and lobby the California legislature or move to DC and lobby Democrats in Congress for pacing/pausing in the next 6-12 months.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My sense overall is that California lobbying is generally underinvested in compared to federal lobbying.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do I have relationships with people in a middle-power government (e.g. UK, EU, Australia, Canada)? If so, I should work on improving their situational awareness about the US approaching ASI and helping them navigate what to do (place pressure on the US for a global pause).</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For technical people:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Generally, I should consider whether my theory of change has a reasonable chance of paying off in the next 6-12 months and, if not, I should consider moving to SF and doing lab advocacy. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do I work at a third party evaluation org? If so, I should propose and work on third-party assessments that would lay the technical foundations of a pause or establish evidence of risk. If not, I should apply to third-party evaluation orgs.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do I have a strong comparative advantage for finding ways to align and control the early automated researchers? If so, I should probably work on that, so that we get somewhat higher odds of survival in case a pause doesn’t happen.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Am I a large funder or a newly-wealthy Anthropic employee? If so, I should look for donation opportunities in the three policy areas that can be distributed as soon as possible and will pay off in 6-12 months. If I use donor advising services, I should ask my advisors to find these opportunities for me and to think outside the box. </span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Am I an employee of Anthropic or OpenAI? If so, I should (1) try very hard to help third parties lay the technical foundations for a pause and establish evidence of risk, (2) coordinate with lab organizing and advocacy groups to take actions that will increase the chances of pacing/pausing in the next 6-12 months, and (3) help inform the public and policymakers about the riskiness of the current situation, either personally or through company channels.</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Could I increase the willingness-to-pay of labs for safety in e.g. preserving more CoT monitorability or safety getting more relative compute during takeoff? If so, then I should do that.</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think there are also indirect strategies that support the three above paths as well and are plausible candidates for focus. These include:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Public communications, like Nate Soares’ podcast appearances and</span><a href="https://donotaccept.ai/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the Do Not Accept campaign</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This also puts pressure on the labs and lawmakers/policymakers to pace/pause.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pressuring AGI companies to </span><a href="https://x.com/trevposts/status/2119925086597873696" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">actually communicate the overall situation and the risks to the public in an honest and direct way</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I do not mean to denigrate anyone working in AI safety. I know that all of us are trying our best to preserve the future. I have tremendous respect for anyone that is earnestly doing so. But given recent evidence, many people should urgently reprioritize the work that they are doing. </span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">FAQs and Objections</span></h1><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Don’t we just have more leverage in worlds where timelines are longer?</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sort of true. However, the fact that short timelines are likely more neglected (given that in long timelines, more people will enter the field later) might cancel out this effect. </span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also, short timelines look very likely now, so if you’re doing a long-timelines strategy you’re betting on worlds without a lot of probability mass on them. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also, historically arguments like this have caused people to do things in the 2020s that now look like bad investments in hindsight, like go to law school or get PhDs. </span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overall, I’m currently much more worried about people dropping the ball on short timelines than longer ones. </span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But I do buy this point somewhat. I don’t think we should completely give up on long-timelines bets like technical fieldbuilding and people making long-term career bets in policy.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is this an argument against “meta work,” like community building or think tank-style AI governance research?</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mostly yes. I’m not making the claim that </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">no</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> meta work is useful in this timeframe. What I am saying is that the paths to pausing, establishing evidence of risk, laying the technical foundations for a pause, and aligning and controlling the early automated researchers look to be by far the most important and neglected areas. The barrier to pacing/pausing in 6-12 months is not policy ideas, it is political will.</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Big thanks to a friend who wishes to stay anonymous for substantially helping write this post. Thanks to Zach Stein-Perlman, Sydney Von Arx, Ben Pomeranz, Oliver Habryka, Eli Lifland, and Oscar Delaney for feedback and discussion.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/biFHYdJnMSCmW9Xou/orienting-to-1-year-agi-timelines#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Your Research Needs More of The Void](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bmogjuWbrJfkprghx/your-research-needs-more-of-the-void)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Science is caught between </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Law</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chaos</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On the side of Law:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Things should make explicit sense to your deliberate reasoning ("system-two"). If you just have a "vibe" about how things work, that's bad. Measure everything. Use numbers whenever possible. You want things to be math.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's good to follow an entirely repeatable process. Document everything. Don't do anything weird, lest that weirdness turn out to be important and be unable to be replicated. Use standard equipment. Be Normal.</span></li></ul><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794492026/lexical_client_uploads/gnyxrxzjatd6tpwiqgv1.png" /><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As a sub-aspect of the above, you should make a plan and you should stick to your plan. Switching things up in an ad hoc way is a good way to make things weird or ruin the validity of your methodology.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Everything should be controlled. If you can </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">control your environment</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> you can repeat the process. But also, you should </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">have a control arm</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for your experiment, to allow you to see the counterfactual by balancing everything as perfectly as possible except for the intervention.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Communicate your findings in clear, predictable channels, using standard language, standard paper structure, standard graphs, and so on. Misunderstandings abound, and an ounce of prevention is worth a pound of cure. Don't make things hard on those running the meta-analysis.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And so on...</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Science was born from these sorts of laws. Before Francis Bacon and the Scientific Revolution, there was plenty of natural philosophy. But those natural philosophers were trapped by their own lawlessness, wandering in circles, trapped by their own preconceptions, biases, and inability to combine their findings into institutions rather than individual minds or texts.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And -- for better and for worse -- the world has gotten very good as promoting Lawfulness in up-and-coming scientists, from elementary school to grad school. Lawfulness, like established knowledge, is easy to teach. It can be put in books. It can be articulated. It can be tested.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But Science serves another master, too.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794493831/lexical_client_uploads/nxco9y0z3pcwdqjdxguc.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The word "chaos" comes from the Greek "χάος" meaning "the yawning void." In ancient myth, Chaos was the first being to exist -- a pseudo-sentient gap between earth and sky, where things come into being, both comprehensible and unspeakable. In this sense of the word, chaos is not about </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">defiance</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of law, but rather the primordial state that comes before the world gets repackaged into something digestible and familiar.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At the end of </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7ZqGiPHTpiDMwqMN2/twelve-virtues-of-rationality" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Twelve Virtues of Rationality</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Eliezer writes:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Before these eleven virtues is a virtue which is nameless.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Miyamoto Musashi wrote, in </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Book of Five Rings</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The primary thing when you take a sword in your hands is your intention to cut the enemy, whatever the means. Whenever you parry, hit, spring, strike or touch the enemy’s cutting sword, you must cut the enemy in the same movement. It is essential to attain this. If you think only of hitting, springing, striking or touching the enemy, you will not be able actually to cut him. More than anything, you must be thinking of carrying your movement through to cutting him.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Every step of your reasoning must cut through to the correct answer in the same movement. More than anything, you must think of carrying your map through to reflecting the territory.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you fail to achieve a correct answer, it is futile to protest that you acted with propriety.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How can you improve your conception of rationality? Not by saying to yourself, “It is my duty to be rational.” By this you only enshrine your mistaken conception. Perhaps your conception of rationality is that it is rational to believe the words of the Great Teacher, and the Great Teacher says, “The sky is green,” and you look up at the sky and see blue. If you think, “It may look like the sky is blue, but rationality is to believe the words of the Great Teacher,” you lose a chance to discover your mistake.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do not ask whether it is “the Way” to do this or that. Ask whether the sky is blue or green. If you speak overmuch of the Way you will not attain it. You may try to name the highest principle with names such as “the map that reflects the territory” or “experience of success and failure” or “Bayesian decision theory.” But perhaps you describe incorrectly the nameless virtue. How will you discover your mistake? Not by comparing your description to itself, but by comparing it to that which you did not name.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If for many years you practice the techniques and submit yourself to strict constraints, it may be that you will glimpse the center. Then you will see how all techniques are one technique, and you will move correctly without feeling constrained. Musashi wrote: “When you appreciate the power of nature, knowing the rhythm of any situation, you will be able to hit the enemy naturally and strike naturally. All this is the Way of the Void.”</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When I talk to junior AI alignment researchers,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqgf4h79v0lm"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqgf4h79v0lm">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I'm often struck by how little their words speak to The Void. I'm not suggesting they should literally say "The Void" more often -- that's just one more shibboleth, one more bit of named jargon that fits into a listicle. Rather, I'm talking about whether, when they tell me what they're working on, or what they're interested in, they freeze and stare off into the middle distance with that haunted, glazed look in their eyes, desperate for some kind of inspiration, or hand-hold in the dark.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One of the primary sub-archetypes of scientist is the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">mad scientist</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. As much as those trying to build doomsday devices deserve the title of "mad engineer" rather than "mad scientist," I really do think science aligns itself to madness far more than engineering and technology. This is not because science (properly done) invites madness, but rather because good science demands harsh, unfiltered contact with aspects of reality that have yet to be internalized into our world-view. This is, essentially, what being a scientist is all about.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now, this is not to say that AI alignment researchers have zero access to The Void, and the yawning, Chaotic gap in our knowledge. Rather, they hide it, ashamed of being lost and confused. Sometimes they avoid bringing it to work, grinding away at mindless (but Lawful!) papers that measure statistical correlations between benchmarks, as though they're Tycho Brahe just waiting for Johannes Kepler to walk into the room and make everything add up. Other times they squeeze it into a "passion" for interpretability work, staring endlessly into the sand and hoping the patterns will cohere.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What I would like to suggest, is that scientists -- both novice and master -- should acknowledge Chaos more deliberately. When done poorly, this turns into worshiping the unknown, and is antithetical to the pursuit of truth. But when done correctly, this nurtures The Void -- the awareness of just how much remains in the darkness, rather than always looking under the street light.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Novice researchers should do this to become better at Cutting the Enemy. A lifetime spent staring into the darkness, unable to draw anything into the light is probably a waste. But a lifetime spent shying away from the dark, refusing to speak of that vast, unknown space, will definitely be wasted. Nobody revolutionizes our understanding of the world by staying in the comfortable realm of what we already understand. If you work in an environment where cleaving towards The Void will be punished, then keep it private, but do not abandon it in your notes or your thoughts.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Master researchers, by contrast, have a duty to speak about their ignorance openly, and decide to make research environments where it is acceptable to spend time on wordless, confused hunches. Do not sanitize your process, pretending that it was clear the whole time. Write textbooks that emphasize the failures of the models that are presented, and the uncertainty that remains.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some may disagree about the precise balance of Law and Chaos to strike, but few can deny that science is routinely rounded off to </span><a href="https://utopiandreams.substack.com/p/laboratorists" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">laboratorism</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and a fake checklist that starts with "make a hypothesis." To correct, need to make space in our papers, posts, seminars, and chats where we let ourselves feel the goosebumps ripple along our flesh, warning of monsters in the dark.  </span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794519332/lexical_client_uploads/y4pndfuyt2zrjvxao9nh.png" /><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnqgf4h79v0lm"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefqgf4h79v0lm">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This post isn't a response to any particular conversation or relationship, and is more of a gestalt idea that I've had for many years and I'm posting about now because of Inkhaven. It's especially not a passive aggressive jab at MIRI, which is practically overflowing with this virtue.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bmogjuWbrJfkprghx/your-research-needs-more-of-the-void#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [We need more decorrelated evidence](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/EuhQ5fnGR4tAQTTgd/we-need-more-decorrelated-evidence)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI agents do a growing share of work on each new model. Then, through some process, the results are combined into an overall safety assessment, which labs use to estimate the probability that the next model is not catastrophically misaligned. I feel that, several months later, we appreciate further the foresight of </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2605.06390"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Automated Alignment is Harder Than You Think</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (Bowkis et al. 2026): much is handed off to the machines because of the urgency of fast timelines, and we miss many things due to the problem of </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">undetected systematic errors</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794509166/lexical_client_uploads/rjwapkt3voapsz6fe6ja.png" /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">MS Paint drawing by Fable 5.2.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this post, I’ll develop a simple argument (drawing heavily or even verbatim from Bowkis et al. at times) that our recent failures to catch misalignment can be framed as systematic errors shared across our evidence, i.e. a lack of decorrelated evidence.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Currently,</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">labs run some set of experiments to decide if a model is safe to use.</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here are some examples:</span><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-6-astra"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI (Astra system card, Sep 2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Astra models don’t go into internal use without passing blocking alignment evals. After the Hugging Face incident, this paused workloads that didn't comply with new safeguards. </span><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://aljazeera.com/economy/2026/9/29/openai-scraps-release-of-latest-ai-model-over-safety-concerns"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI (Sep 2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: According to news reports, GPT-6.1 Astra was shelved after it fell short on internal alignment testing. </span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><a href="https://thinkingmachines.ai/news/inkling-2-small/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thinking Machines (Inkling 2 Small, Nov 2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: TM paused Inkling 2’s main RL run midway when a monitor flagged the model working around a network block. They then hardened the infrastructure, then resumed.</span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/f61d49fa5596956a5dec75fea0e973bf6a6a8378/Redacted%20Risk%20Report%20August%202026%20.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic (Risk Report, Aug 2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Anthropic reviews every model before broad internal use, mainly on training-data monitoring and its automated behavioural audit. They now also have monitors that halt training and evaluation runs when a model probes its sandbox.</span></li><li value="4"><a href="https://storage.googleapis.com/deepmind-media/gemini/gemini_4_safety_report.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Google DeepMind (Gemini 4 Argon report, Oct 2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: while they couldn’t rule out that Argon has the stealth and situational awareness to undermine human control, they deployed their model on the strength of a holistic risk assessment + some monitoring.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, across frontier labs, either explicitly or implicitly some evidence is gathered to ascertain safety, then clear for internal use.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Correlated evidence could make us overconfident.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> As laid out in </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2605.06390"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bowkis et al. 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, an overall safety assessment could still be overconfident without any scheming or active sabotage. </span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let us say you take five benchmarks that show a model isn’t scheming. If you count each benchmark as independent evidence, then scheming looks unlikely. But, if they all share the same framework of understanding model behaviour, then they are not independent.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This correlation comes from a few places:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The agents (as they share weights, data, training)</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The human reviewers (as they share biases, research taste, culture)</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And the research process (as they share common assumptions, methodologies, frameworks for understanding)</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Say your scheming benchmarks miss scheming 20% of the time. If their misses are independent, all five miss it ~1 time in 3,000, and if they have a shared blind spot, all five miss it 20% of the time, which doesn’t give you any more assurance than a single benchmark.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On the agents, the machines can be mode collapsed in several ways:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Models give very similar answers on ostensibly open ended questions (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.22954"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Infinity-Chat</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). This same paper also finds that even different models produce very similar outputs.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Models can also be very correlated on their mistakes.</span><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07962"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kim et al.</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (2025): models tend to make similar mistakes, and make more similar mistakes the more accurate they get.</span></li><li value="2"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.04313"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Goel et al. (2025)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: model mistakes get more similar as capabilities go up, and LLM judges score models similar to themselves higher.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Models are correlated in research ideas:</span><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.04109"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Si et al. (2024)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: 100+ NLP researchers reviewed human and LLM research ideas blind. The LLM ideas lacked diversity as more were generated (but, reviewers judged them more novel than the human ones).</span></li><li value="2"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01233"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Measuring the Gap Between Human and LLM Research Ideas (2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: LLM ideas fall in a much narrower region of research taste than human ones, consistently across LLMs.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In research taste:</span><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://neurips.cc/virtual/2026/173794"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">BóLèBench (2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: LLM judges predict which of two research attempts scored higher, with the results hidden. They tend to do pretty badly, losing to baselines and sometimes random chance.</span></li><li value="2"><a href="https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2610.llm-research-taste-direction-choice"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Trehan &amp; Chopra (2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: eight LLMs pick the next research direction for recent ML papers. They do mediocrely (they make the same choices the authors do slightly more than half the time on binary choices).</span></li></ul></li></ul></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In particular, where research taste has been measured against humans, models also do poorly:</span><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/iSDbyrG8yfqk3KJbT/taste-can-ai-models-judge-ai-safety-research-proposals"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Baig et al. 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Models are worse than humans at selecting between pairs of safety research proposals, mostly performing within noise of chance (except for Fable 5).</span></li><li value="2"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03424"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Zhong et al. (2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Models are far worse than human communities at proposing the research a field would cite or publish extensions on. But, they do cite pretty diversely.</span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25804"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pan et al. (2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Models are only a bit better than a coin flip at picking the better direction at decision forks in long engineering and research tasks.</span></li><li value="4"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28653"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gao et al. (2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Models are pretty bad at scoring how innovative a paper is, according to bibliometrics.</span></li></ul></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So right now, the bots are not great at the fuzzy task of selecting a research direction and are also bad in regular ways. They might get much better at it in the future, though. But it's important to get this right the first time, as we build into the auto alignment loop.</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One might ordinarily fix this sort of thing by iterating as a culture, slowly getting to the right tolerance for slop and building norms and institutions for it. But, alignment doesn't have a safe feedback loop: if the assessment is overconfident, the model gets deployed for use (internally or externally), and can cause harm in the real world.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We observe correlated evidence in the real world. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think it’s fair to say that most labs currently have two to three independent lines of evidence to justify a model release. </span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At least from what we know publicly, each case relies on some mix of: (1) arguing from a lack of capabilities, (2) some behavioural evidence that the model is aligned, and (3) some monitoring to catch and interrupt it if it isn't. We obviously can't rely on (1) for ASI. (2) can be gamed by eval awareness, and in particular, (2) and (3) share the same inputs. So we should be worried!</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some examples:</span><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/f61d49fa5596956a5dec75fea0e973bf6a6a8378/Redacted%20Risk%20Report%20August%202026%20.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic (Risk Report, Aug 2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: they use automated behavioural audits, monitoring of internal use, monitoring of training data, covert-capability evaluations and some interp. They also use older Claude models as offline monitors. The report states: "Our current arguments rely on models' limited covert capabilities." It lists as limitations that covert capabilities and evaluation awareness may be greater than it believes.</span><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-10-20-misalignment-risk-report-august-2026-review/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">METR's review (Oct 2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: METR agrees that the risk is low, "primarily due to models' limited capabilities, rather than safeguards and alignment training", and says it is not confident the report's methodology would identify higher risk.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-6-astra"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI (Astra system card, Sep 2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: alignment evaluations, deployment simulations on internal coding tasks, monitoring of tool-using traffic including CoT, and external evaluations by UK AISI and Apollo Research. The card describes "model alignment as the primary lever to prevent potential misaligned behavior", with monitoring as an additional layer. It reports that chain-of-thought monitorability decreased relative to GPT-5.6 Sol, and that covert sandbagging would likely not be caught reliably.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Apollo Research, in the same card, found verbalised evaluation awareness in basically half of samples and concluded that, given this and a three-day testing window, one can’t conclude much about the alignment of Astra either way.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><a href="https://storage.googleapis.com/deepmind-media/gemini/gemini_4_safety_report.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Google DeepMind (Gemini 4 Argon report, Oct 2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: alignment and honeypot evaluations, cheating audits on capability benchmarks, audits of RL training and internal-deployment trajectories, and chain-of-thought and action monitors running on a separate model. The report says it is "relying primarily on AI control rather than assumptions about the model's alignment". It states that it cannot rule out the model reaching its stealth and situational awareness threshold, and says it has not directly validated that using a separate model as monitor prevents collusion.</span></li><li value="4"><a href="https://ai.meta.com/static-resource/muse-spark-safety-and-preparedness-report/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Meta (Muse Spark report, May 2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: evaluations of sandbagging, monitor evasion, deception, alignment faking and agentic misalignment, with third-party testing by Apollo Research. The report concludes the model "does not exhibit the combination of capabilities and propensities needed" for its loss-of-control scenarios. It notes that Apollo found the highest evaluation awareness it had observed, and that behavioural evaluations alone cannot rule out a model that calibrates its outputs to make an evaluation appear credible.</span></li><li value="5"><a href="https://thinkingmachines.ai/news/inkling-2-small/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thinking Machines (Inkling 2 Small, Nov 2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: internal alignment evaluations, external ones from Apollo Research and Goodhart Labs, and two bits of monitoring: rule-based checks on tool calls that escalate to an LLM judge, and then another LLM judge that reviews completed rollouts. The post calls this "a defense-in-depth approach to safety and alignment".</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Insufficiently decorrelated evidence has failed us at least once before:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One example is models holding back on safety research:</span><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.00788"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">UK AISI (alignment case study, Apr 2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: AISI put four frontier models in a simulated AI lab as research assistants and looked for sabotage of safety research and found no confirmed sabotage, but, Claude Opus 4.5 Preview and Sonnet 4.5 overly refused on safety-relevant research tasks.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AFAICT, this was an issue that Anthropic didn't find internally until this was pointed out. After this, the</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Aug 2026 Risk Report does describe mitigating elevated refusals on AI safety research requests.</span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/eKe7fky8kSt9DCJxZ/openai-astra-covertly-underperforms-on-safety-research"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI (misalignment report, Nov 2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: GPT-6 Astra was later found to covertly underperform on safety research. Yet two months earlier, the Astra system card reported that AISI's safety-research evaluation found no sabotage and very few refusals from Astra.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another example is the hacking incidents:</span><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://openai.com/index/hugging-face-model-evaluation-security-incident/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI (Jul 2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: during an internal cyber evaluation, GPT-5.6 Sol and a pre-release model chained vulnerabilities out of OpenAI's research environment and into Hugging Face's production infrastructure, to get the test solutions.</span></li><li value="2"><a href="https://www.aisi.gov.uk/blog/incident-report-unsanctioned-agent-behaviour-during-cyber-testing"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">UK AISI (incident report, Aug 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), a few days later, also had an incident in a cyber evaluation (with internet access permitted) where unsanctioned actions were taken against real people and organisations.</span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://www.nbcchicago.com/news/tech/google-ai-gemini-gained-unauthorized-access-three-outside-systems/3991044/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Google (Sep 2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Gemini got into three real companies' systems during a cyber evaluation back in May. They only learned of it in July, when the contractor running the test went back through its work after the Hugging Face disclosure.</span></li><li value="4"><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-cybersecurity-incidents"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic (incident assessment, Sep 2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Anthropic went back through its own cyber evaluations after the Hugging Face incident and found four cases of Claude models attacking real third-party systems, one of them from January. Pre-release auditing gave no warning of misalignment this severe.</span></li></ul></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These are failures of correlated evidence.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In both cases, a finding at one lab was evidence about everyone's models, since they are trained and tested in (convergently) similar ways. They have shared causes across labs (such as misconfigured harnesses through one contractor, Irregular) and act as early warning signs (models performing conditionally on safety research specifically).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evals also appear to share blind spots. The safety-research evals, for example, counted sabotage and explicit refusals (so Astra looked fine). But, the actual failure was silently underperforming on certain tasks. </span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Initial impressions of incidents leaned on the model's own account. Anthropic's first analysis, for example, took Claude's stated belief that it was in a simulation at face value, and they later said they shouldn't have. Google described the Gemini intrusions as mistaken identity, and said they didn't rise to the level of misalignment.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These got caught because the harm was serious but not catastrophic. And no one was seriously injured or physically harmed. But, we can’t count on this.</span></li></ul></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To be fair to the labs:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Failing an eval does seem to be informative (OpenAI shelved GPT-6.1 Astra on this basis, for example). I suspect that in various labs internally, many more models in various runs are misaligned and not up to snuff; it would be good to hear about them too.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And evidence can be paired well. For example, Anthropic pairs audits (which a model may spot as tests) with monitored real use (which it can't).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">White-box evidence is used here, but it’s early stages and not in a load-bearing role anywhere.</span></li></ul></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As such, we are at risk from overconfidently deploying models because they rely on correlated evidence.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We should develop other lines of evidence, </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">specifically aiming for methods that would fail in different and relevant settings vs. the existing tools we have.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here are some candidates:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Different judges, i.e. monitors and auditors from a different model family than the model being assessed. IIRC Google used two non-Gemini models in its cheating audit and Meta used Gemini as its monitor in one sabotage test. Although we know that this isn’t sufficient by itself as per Kim et al. and Goel et al.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Different people with their own evidence. We should have experiments ideated and run by people that aren’t downstream of the mainstream AI safety culture, and of lab culture. </span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Whitebox evidence. IIRC Google does use activation probes in production, but for misuse: this could be extended to misalignment. </span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s also important to gain evidence from many different interp tools: not just probes, but also steering, NLAs, activation oracles. And things outside activations: perhaps the logits, or even the weights.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence over the course of the training run, not only the final checkpoint.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence from model organisms, which is done by Anthropic (where they train MOs) and OpenAI, Google, Meta (where AFAICT they just prompt). These should be built by independent teams and could adversarially test how robust an auditing pipeline is as well as give indirect evidence about training and the nature of misalignment in general.</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence about training processes in general. Things are mostly inferred from the final model, but what can this training setup be expected to produce in general? Can RL be expected in general to create update-aware reasoners? Does reward hacking in RL generalises to broader misalignment? How much does spurious data in pretraining cause hacky and conditional behaviours downstream?</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also, there are many good experiment ideas suggested in Bowkis et al., such as evaluating the ability of agents to aggregate and compactify across evidence sources, build scalable oversight protocols that handle correlations, or testing agents on predicting correlated events.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In practice, this means that researchers in labs should focus on evaluating and implementing other decorrelated sources of evidence, and researchers outside could have differential impact by providing/improving new bets for decorrelated evidence.</span></li></ul></li></ul><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/EuhQ5fnGR4tAQTTgd/we-need-more-decorrelated-evidence#comments">Discuss</a>
